**William Jefferson  Clinton** ([né](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_name#Maiden_and_married_names) **Blythe III**; born August 19, 1946) is an American retired politician and lawyer  who served as the 42nd [president of the United States](24113.md) from 1993 to 2001. A member of the [Democratic Party](5043544.md), he previously served as the [attorney general of Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_general_of_Arkansas) from 1977 to 1979, the [governor of Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Arkansas) from 1979 to 1981, and governor again from 1983 to 1992. His centrist [Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way) political philosophy became known as [Clintonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clintonism), which defined [his presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bill_Clinton) and the succeeding decades of [Democratic Party into the 21st century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\)).

Born and raised in [Arkansas](1930.md), Clinton graduated from [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University) in 1968, and later from [Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School), where he met his future wife, [Hillary Rodham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton). After graduating from law school, Clinton returned to Arkansas and won election as state attorney general, followed by [two non-consecutive tenures as Arkansas governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorships_of_Bill_Clinton). As governor, he overhauled the state's education system and served as [chairman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chair_\(officer\)) of the [National Governors Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Governors_Association). Running as a "[New Democrat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrats_\(United_States\))", Clinton was elected president in the [1992 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election), defeating the incumbent [Republican](32070.md) president [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush), and the independent businessman [Ross Perot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Perot). He became the first president to be born in the [Baby Boomer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_Boomer) generation and the [youngest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_age) to serve two full terms.

Clinton presided over the longest American period of peacetime economic expansion at the time. He signed into law the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement), the [Brady Handgun Violence Prevention Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brady_Handgun_Violence_Prevention_Act) and the [Violent Crime Control and Law Enforcement Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act) but failed to pass his plan for [national health care reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_health_care_plan_of_1993), and accepted a controversial [compromise policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell) to his original plan to allow gay and lesbian service members to serve openly in the military. Starting in the mid-1990s, after Democrats were soundly defeated in the [1994 midterms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_United_States_elections), he began an ideological evolution as he became much more conservative in his domestic policy, advocating for and signing the [Personal Responsibility and Work Opportunity Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_Responsibility_and_Work_Opportunity_Act) and Republican-led financial deregulation measures, while still championing liberal policy such as the [State Children's Health Insurance Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Children's_Health_Insurance_Program). He appointed [Ruth Bader Ginsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Bader_Ginsburg) and [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer) to the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md). In foreign policy, Clinton ordered U.S. military intervention in the [Bosnian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosnian_War) and [Kosovo wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosovo_war), eventually signing the [Dayton Peace agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayton_Agreement). He also called for the [expansion of NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_of_NATO) in Eastern Europe and many former [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) members joined NATO during his presidency. Clinton's foreign policy in the Middle East saw him sign the [Iraq Liberation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraq_Liberation_Act) which gave aid to groups against [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein). He also participated in the [Oslo I Accord](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_I_Accord) and [Camp David Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Camp_David_Summit) to advance the [Israeli–Palestinian peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israeli%E2%80%93Palestinian_peace_process), and assisted the [Northern Ireland peace process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_peace_process).

Clinton won re-election in the [1996 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_presidential_election), defeating Republican nominee [Bob Dole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dole) and returning [Reform Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Party_of_the_United_States_of_America) nominee Ross Perot. In his second term, Clinton made use of [permanent normal trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_normal_trade_relations). Many of his second term accomplishments were overshadowed by [his highly publicised affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton%E2%80%93Lewinsky_scandal) with [White House](33057.md) intern [Monica Lewinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monica_Lewinsky), when it was revealed in early 1998 that the two had been engaging in an eighteen-month-long sexual relationship.[^cite_note-1] This scandal escalated throughout the year, culminating in December when [Clinton was impeached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_was_impeached) by the [House of Representatives](19468510.md), becoming the first U.S. president to be impeached since [Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson). The two impeachment articles that the House passed were centered around [perjury](23688.md) and Clinton using the powers of the presidency to commit obstruction of justice. In January 1999, [Clinton's impeachment trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton's_impeachment_trial) began in the [Senate](24909346.md), where he was acquitted two months later on both charges. During the last three years of Clinton's presidency, the [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office) reported a budget surplus—the first and only such surplus since 1969.

Clinton left office in 2001 with the joint-highest approval rating of any U.S. president. His presidency consistently ranks among the middle to upper tier in [historical rankings of U.S. presidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_U.S._presidents). His personal conduct and [misconduct allegations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton_sexual_assault_and_misconduct_allegations) have made him the subject of substantial scrutiny. Since leaving office, Clinton has been involved in public speaking and humanitarian work. He created the [Clinton Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Foundation) to address international causes such as the prevention of HIV/AIDS and global warming. In 2009, he was named the [United Nations special envoy to Haiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Envoy_of_the_Secretary-General). After the [2010 Haiti earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Haiti_earthquake), Clinton founded the [Clinton Bush Haiti Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_Bush_Haiti_Fund) with [George W. Bush](3414021.md). He has remained active in Democratic Party politics, campaigning for his wife's [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton_2008_presidential_campaign) and [2016 presidential campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton_2016_presidential_campaign). Following [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter)'s [death in December 2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Jimmy_Carter), he is the earliest-serving living former U.S. president and the last surviving president to have served in the 20th century.[^cite_note-2]

## Early life and career



[Clinton's birthplace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_William_Jefferson_Clinton_Birthplace_Home_National_Historic_Site) home in [Hope, Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope,_Arkansas)

Clinton was born as William Jefferson Blythe III on August 19, 1946, at the Julia Chester Hospital in [Hope, Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hope,_Arkansas).[^cite_note-3] He is the son of [William Jefferson Blythe Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Jefferson_Blythe_Jr.), a traveling salesman who died in an automobile accident three months before his birth, and [Virginia Dell Cassidy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Clinton_Kelley) (later Virginia Kelley).[^cite_note-whitehouse.gov_bio-4]

Clinton's parents married on September 4, 1943, but this union later proved bigamous, as Blythe was still married to his fourth wife.[^cite_note-5] Virginia traveled to [New Orleans](53842.md) to study nursing soon after Bill was born, leaving him in Hope with her parents Eldridge and Edith Cassidy, who owned and ran a small grocery store.[^cite_note-My_Life-6] At a time when the southern United States was [racially segregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation_in_the_United_States), Clinton's grandparents sold goods on [credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_\(finance\)) to people of all races.[^cite_note-11] In 1950, Bill's mother returned from nursing school and married [Roger Clinton Sr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Clinton_Sr.), who co-owned an [automobile dealership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car_dealership) in [Hot Springs, Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_Springs,_Arkansas), with his brother and [Earl T. Ricks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earl_T._Ricks).[^cite_note-My_Life-6] The family moved to Hot Springs in 1950.[^cite_note-12] Although he immediately assumed use of his stepfather's surname, it was not until Clinton turned 15[^cite_note-Oprah_Talks_to_Bill_Clinton-13] that he formally adopted the surname Clinton as a gesture toward him.[^cite_note-My_Life-6] Clinton has described his stepfather as a gambler and an alcoholic who regularly abused his family. The physical abuse only ceased after a then-14-year-old Bill challenged his stepfather to "stand and face" him, though the verbal abuse continued.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14] Bill would eventually forgive Roger Sr. for his abusive actions near the latter's death.[^cite_note-Holmes-15][^cite_note-Levin-16]

In Hot Springs, Clinton attended St. John's Catholic Elementary School, Ramble Elementary School, and [Hot Springs High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_Springs_High_School_\(Arkansas\)), which was [segregated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_segregation_in_the_United_States), where he was an active student leader, avid reader, and musician.[^cite_note-My_Life-6] Clinton was in the chorus and played the [tenor saxophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenor_saxophone), winning first chair in the state band's saxophone section. While in high school, Clinton performed for two years in a jazz trio, The 3 Kings, with [Randy Goodrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Goodrum), who became a successful professional pianist.[^cite_note-17]



Clinton in [Hot Springs High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_Springs_High_School_\(Arkansas\))'s 1963 yearbook

In 1961, Clinton became a member of the Hot Springs Chapter of the [Order of DeMolay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DeMolay_International), a youth group affiliated with [Freemasonry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freemasonry), but he never became a Freemason.[^cite_note-18] He briefly considered dedicating his life to music, but felt that his musical skills would never match the skills of the best musicians, so pursued politics instead.[^cite_note-My_Life-6] Clinton began an interest in law at Hot Springs High when he took up the challenge to argue the defense of the ancient Roman senator [Catiline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catiline) in a mock trial in his Latin class.[^cite_note-19] After a vigorous defense that made use of his "budding rhetorical and political skills", he told the Latin teacher Elizabeth Buck it "made him realize that someday he would study law".[^cite_note-20]

Clinton has identified two influential moments in his life, both occurring in 1963, that contributed to his decision to become a public figure. One was his visit as a [Boys Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boys_Nation) senator to the [White House](33057.md) to meet President [John F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy).[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14] The other was watching [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md)'s "[I Have a Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Have_a_Dream)" speech on TV, which impressed him so much that he later memorized it.[^cite_note-21]

## College and law school years

### Georgetown University



Clinton ran for president of the [Student Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University_Student_Association) while attending the School of Foreign Service at [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University).

With the aid of scholarships, Clinton attended the [School of Foreign Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walsh_School_of_Foreign_Service) at [Georgetown University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_University) in Washington, D.C., receiving a [Bachelor of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Science) in foreign service in 1968. Georgetown was the only university where Clinton applied.[^cite_note-22]

In 1964 and 1965, Clinton won elections for [class president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class_president).[^cite_note-23] From 1964 to 1967, he was an intern and then a clerk in the office of Arkansas senator [J. William Fulbright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._William_Fulbright).[^cite_note-My_Life-6] While in college, he became a brother of service fraternity [Alpha Phi Omega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_Phi_Omega)[^cite_note-24] and was elected to [Phi Beta Kappa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phi_Beta_Kappa). He is a member of [Kappa Kappa Psi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kappa_Kappa_Psi) honorary band fraternity.[^cite_note-25]

### Oxford

Upon graduating from Georgetown in 1968, Clinton won a [Rhodes Scholarship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhodes_Scholarship) to [University College, Oxford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_College,_Oxford), where he initially read for a [B.Phil.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B.Phil.) in [philosophy, politics, and economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy,_Politics,_and_Economics) but transferred to a [B.Litt.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachelor_of_Letters) in politics and, ultimately, a B.Phil. in politics.[^cite_note-Hoffman-26] Clinton did not expect to return for the second year because of the draft and so he switched programs; this type of activity was common among other Rhodes Scholars from his cohort. He was offered the opportunity to study at [Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School), so he left early to return to the United States and did not receive a degree from Oxford.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14][^cite_note-Dowd-27][^cite_note-28]

Clinton befriended fellow American Rhodes Scholar Frank Aller during his time at Oxford. In 1969, Aller received a [draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draft_lottery_\(1969\)) letter that mandated deployment to the [Vietnam War](32611.md). Aller's 1971 suicide had an influential impact on Clinton.[^cite_note-Hoffman-26][^cite_note-29] British writer and feminist [Sara Maitland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sara_Maitland) said of Clinton, "I remember Bill and Frank Aller taking me to a pub in Walton Street in the summer term of 1969 and talking to me about the Vietnam War. I knew nothing about it, and when Frank began to describe the napalming of civilians I began to cry. Bill said that feeling bad wasn't good enough. That was the first time I encountered the idea that liberal sensitivities weren't enough and you had to do something about such things."[^cite_note-Hoffman-26] Clinton was a member of the [Oxford University Basketball Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_University_Men's_Basketball) and also played for Oxford University's [rugby union](25405.md) team.[^cite_note-30]

While Clinton was president in 1994, he received an honorary [Doctor of Civil Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctor_of_Civil_Law) degree and a fellowship from the [University of Oxford](31797.md), specifically for being "a doughty and tireless champion of the cause of world peace", having "a powerful collaborator in his wife", and for winning "general applause for his achievement of resolving the gridlock that prevented an agreed budget".[^cite_note-Dowd-27][^cite_note-31]

### Vietnam War opposition and draft controversy

During the Vietnam War, Clinton received educational draft deferments while he was in England in 1968 and 1969.[^cite_note-32] While at Oxford, he participated in [Vietnam War protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_the_Vietnam_War) and organized a [Moratorium to End the War in Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moratorium_to_End_the_War_in_Vietnam) event in October 1969.[^cite_note-My_Life-6] He was planning to attend law school in the U.S. and knew he might lose his deferment. Clinton tried unsuccessfully to obtain positions in the [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_of_the_United_States) and the [Air Force officer candidate school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_Force_Officer_Training_School), and he then made arrangements to join the [Reserve Officers' Training Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserve_Officers'_Training_Corps) (ROTC) program at the [University of Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Arkansas).[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34]

He subsequently decided not to join the ROTC, saying in a letter to the officer in charge of the program that he opposed the war, but did not think it was honorable to use ROTC, National Guard, or Reserve service to avoid serving in Vietnam. He further stated that because he opposed the war, he would not volunteer to serve in uniform, but would subject himself to the draft, and would serve if selected only as a way "to maintain my political viability within the system".[^cite_note-35] Clinton registered for the draft and received a high number (311), meaning that those whose birthdays had been drawn as numbers 1 to 310 would be [drafted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Draft_lottery_\(1969\)) before him, making it unlikely he would be called up. (In fact, the highest number drafted was 195.)[^cite_note-36] [Colonel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonel_\(United_States\)) Eugene Holmes, the Army officer involved with Clinton's ROTC application, issued a [notarized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notarize) statement during the 1992 presidential campaign stating that he suspected Clinton attempted to manipulate the situation to avoid the draft.[^cite_note-Morris1999-37]

During the 1992 campaign, it was revealed that Clinton's uncle had attempted to secure him a position in the [Navy Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Navy_Reserve), which would have prevented him from being deployed to Vietnam. This effort was unsuccessful and Clinton said in 1992 that he had been unaware of it until then.[^cite_note-38] Although legal, Clinton's actions with respect to the draft and deciding whether to serve in the military were criticized during his first presidential campaign by conservatives and some Vietnam veterans, some of whom charged that he had used Fulbright's influence to avoid military service.[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] Clinton's 1992 campaign manager, [James Carville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Carville), successfully argued that Clinton's letter in which he declined to join the ROTC should be made public, insisting that voters, many of whom had also opposed the Vietnam War, would understand and appreciate his position.[^cite_note-41]

### Law school

After Oxford, Clinton attended Yale Law School and earned a [Juris Doctor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juris_Doctor) (J.D.) degree in 1973.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14] In 1971, he met his future wife, Hillary Rodham, in the [Yale Law Library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lillian_Goldman_Law_Library); she was a class year ahead of him.[^cite_note-HRCBio-42] They began dating and were soon inseparable. After only about a month, Clinton postponed his summer plans to be a coordinator for the [George McGovern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_McGovern) [campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_McGovern_presidential_campaign,_1972) for the [1972 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_presidential_election) to move in with her in California.[^cite_note-43] The couple continued [living together](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohabitating) in New Haven when they returned to law school.[^cite_note-nys-rad-44]

Clinton eventually [moved to Texas with Rodham in 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_presidential_election_in_Texas#McGovern_campaign) to take a job leading McGovern's effort there. He spent considerable time in [Dallas](53838.md), at the campaign's local headquarters on Lemmon Avenue, where he had an office. Clinton worked with future two-term [mayor of Dallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Dallas) [Ron Kirk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Kirk),[^cite_note-45] future [governor of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_governors_of_Texas) [Ann Richards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ann_Richards),[^cite_note-46] and then unknown television director and filmmaker [Steven Spielberg](26940.md).[^cite_note-47]

## Early political career

Further information: [Electoral history of Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_history_of_Bill_Clinton)

After graduating from [Yale Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yale_Law_School), Clinton returned to Arkansas and became a law professor at the [University of Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Arkansas). In 1974, he ran for the [House of Representatives](19468510.md). Running in the conservative [3rd district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas's_3rd_congressional_district) against incumbent Republican [John Paul Hammerschmidt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Paul_Hammerschmidt), Clinton's campaign was bolstered by the anti-Republican and anti-incumbent mood resulting from the [Watergate scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watergate_scandal). Hammerschmidt, who had received 77% of the vote in 1972, defeated Clinton by only a 52% to 48% margin. In 1976, Clinton ran for [Arkansas attorney general](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_Attorney_General). Defeating the secretary of state and the deputy attorney general in the Democratic primary, Clinton was elected with no opposition in the general election, as no Republican had run for the office.[^cite_note-AllPolitics1-48][^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14]

## Governor of Arkansas (1979–1981, 1983–1992)

Main article: [Governorships of Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorships_of_Bill_Clinton)



Clinton meets with President [Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmy_Carter), 1978.

In 1978, Clinton entered the Arkansas gubernatorial primary. At just 31 years old, he was one of the youngest gubernatorial candidates in the state's history. Clinton was elected [Governor of Arkansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Arkansas) in [1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Arkansas_gubernatorial_election), having defeated the Republican candidate [Lynn Lowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynn_Lowe), a farmer from [Texarkana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Texarkana,_Arkansas). Clinton was only 32 years old when he took office, the youngest governor in the country at the time and the second youngest governor in the history of Arkansas.[^cite_note-49] Due to his youthful appearance, Clinton was often called the "Boy Governor".[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] He worked on educational reform and directed the maintenance of Arkansas's roads, with wife Hillary leading a successful committee on urban health care reform. However, his term included an unpopular motor vehicle tax and citizens' anger over the escape of Cuban refugees (from the [Mariel boatlift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel_boatlift)) detained in [Fort Chaffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fort_Chaffee) in 1980. Monroe Schwarzlose, of [Kingsland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingsland,_Arkansas) in [Cleveland County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_County,_Arkansas), polled 31 percent of the vote against Clinton in the Democratic gubernatorial primary of 1980. Some suggested Schwarzlose's unexpected voter turnout foreshadowed Clinton's defeat by Republican challenger [Frank D. White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_D._White) in the general election that year. As Clinton once joked, he was the youngest ex-governor in the nation's history.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14]

After leaving office in January 1981, Clinton joined friend [Bruce Lindsey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Lindsey)'s Little Rock law firm of Wright, Lindsey and Jennings.[^cite_note-53] In 1982, he was elected governor a second time and kept the office for ten years. Effective with the 1986 election, Arkansas had changed its gubernatorial term of office from two to four years. During his term, he helped transform Arkansas's economy and improved the state's educational system.[^cite_note-pendleton-54] For [senior citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senior_citizen), he removed the [sales tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sales_tax) from medications and increased the home property-tax exemption.[^cite_note-The_Natural-55] He became a leading figure among the [New Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Democrats_\(United_States\)), a group of [Democrats](5043544.md) who advocated welfare reform, smaller government, and other policies not supported by liberals. Formally organized as the [Democratic Leadership Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Leadership_Council) (DLC), the New Democrats argued that in light of President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md)'s [landslide victory in 1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election), the Democratic Party needed to adopt a more centrist political stance in order to succeed at the national level.[^cite_note-The_Natural-55][^cite_note-56] Clinton delivered the [Democratic response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_response_to_the_State_of_the_Union_address) to Reagan's [1985 State of the Union Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_State_of_the_Union_Address) and served as chair of the [National Governors Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Governors_Association) from 1986 to 1987, bringing him to an audience beyond Arkansas.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14]



Governor and [Mrs. Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mrs._Clinton) attend the Dinner Honoring the Nation's Governors in the White House with President Ronald Reagan and first lady [Nancy Reagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Reagan), 1987.

In the early 1980s, Clinton made reform of the Arkansas education system a top priority of his gubernatorial administration. The Arkansas Education Standards Committee was chaired by Clinton's wife Hillary, who was also an attorney as well as the chair of the [Legal Services Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_Services_Corporation). The committee transformed Arkansas's education system. Proposed reforms included more spending for schools (supported by a sales-tax increase), better opportunities for gifted children, vocational education, higher teachers' salaries, more course variety, and compulsory teacher competency exams. The reforms passed in September 1983 after Clinton called a special [legislative session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_General_Assembly)—the longest in Arkansas history.[^cite_note-pendleton-54] Many have considered this the greatest achievement of the Clinton governorship.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14][^cite_note-The_Natural-55] He defeated four Republican candidates for governor: Lowe (1978), White (1982 and 1986), [Jonesboro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonesboro,_Arkansas) businessmen [Woody Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woody_Freeman) (1984), and [Sheffield Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheffield_Nelson) of Little Rock (1990).[^cite_note-AllPolitics1-48]

Also in the 1980s, the Clintons' personal and business affairs included transactions that became the basis of the [Whitewater controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewater_controversy) investigation, which later dogged his presidential administration.[^cite_note-Clinton_Wars-57] After extensive investigation over several years, no indictments were made against the Clintons related to the years in Arkansas.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14][^cite_note-The_Survivor-58]

According to some sources, Clinton was a [death penalty](412425.md) opponent in his early years, but he eventually switched positions.[^cite_note-mbsrgd-59][^cite_note-60] However he might have felt previously, by 1992, Clinton was insisting that Democrats "should no longer feel guilty about protecting the innocent".[^cite_note-Hartman2015-61] During Clinton's final term as governor, [Arkansas performed its first executions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Arkansas) since 1964 (the death penalty had been reinstated in 1976).[^cite_note-62] As governor, he oversaw the [first four executions carried out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_people_executed_in_Arkansas) by the state of Arkansas since the death penalty was reinstated there in 1976: one by [electric chair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_chair) and three by [lethal injection](62745.md).[^cite_note-63] To draw attention to his stance on capital punishment, Clinton flew home to Arkansas mid-campaign in 1992, in order to affirm in person that the controversial execution of [Ricky Ray Rector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricky_Ray_Rector) would go forward as scheduled.[^cite_note-Applebome1992-64][^cite_note-65]

### Scandals and allegations

During his time as governor in the 1980s, Arkansas was the center of a drug smuggling operation through [Mena Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mena_Intermountain_Municipal_Airport). [CIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA) agent [Barry Seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Seal) allegedly imported three to five billion dollars' worth of [cocaine](7701.md) through the airport, and the operation was linked to the [Iran–Contra affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Contra_affair).[^cite_note-66] Clinton was accused of knowing about this operation, although nothing could be proven against him.[^cite_note-67][^cite_note-68] Journalist [Sam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Smith_\(journalist\)) tied him to various questionable business dealings.[^cite_note-69] Clinton was also accused by [Gennifer Flowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennifer_Flowers) to have used cocaine as governor[^cite_note-70] and his half-brother Roger was sentenced to prison in 1985 for possession and smuggling of cocaine, but was later [pardoned by his brother](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton_pardon_controversy) after serving his sentence.[^cite_note-71] During his time in Arkansas, there were also other scandals such as the [Whitewater controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewater_controversy)[^cite_note-72] involving the Clintons' real estate dealings, and Bill Clinton was accused of serious sexual misconduct in Arkansas, including allegations of using the [Arkansas State Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkansas_State_Police) to gain access to women ([Troopergate affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troopergate_\(Bill_Clinton\))).[^cite_note-73] The [killing of Don Henry and Kevin Ives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Killing_of_Don_Henry_and_Kevin_Ives) in 1987 started various [conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_body_count_conspiracy_theory) that accused Clinton and the Arkansas state authorities of covering up the crime.[^cite_note-74]

### 1988 Democratic presidential primaries



Clinton in 1986

In 1987, the media speculated that Clinton would enter the presidential race. Clinton decided to remain as Arkansas governor (following consideration for the potential candidacy of Hillary for governor, initially favored—but ultimately vetoed—by the First Lady).[^cite_note-autogenerated1-75] For the nomination, Clinton endorsed [Massachusetts governor](252510.md) [Michael Dukakis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Dukakis). He gave the nationally televised opening night address at the [1988 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_Democratic_National_Convention), but his speech, which was 33 minutes long and twice the length it was expected to be, was criticized for being too long.[^cite_note-76] Clinton presented himself both as a moderate and as a member of the New Democrat wing of the Democratic Party, and he headed the moderate Democratic Leadership Council in 1990 and 1991.[^cite_note-The_Natural-55][^cite_note-The_Choice-77]

## Presidential campaigns

### 1992 presidential candidacy

Main articles: [Bill Clinton 1992 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton_1992_presidential_campaign), [1992 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), and [1992 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election)

In the first primary contest, the [Iowa Caucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iowa_caucuses), Clinton finished a distant third to Iowa senator [Tom Harkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Harkin). During the campaign for the [New Hampshire primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Hampshire_primary), reports surfaced that Clinton had engaged in an extramarital affair with [Gennifer Flowers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gennifer_Flowers). Clinton fell far behind former Massachusetts senator [Paul Tsongas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Tsongas) in the New Hampshire polls.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14] Following [Super Bowl XXVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XXVI), Clinton and his wife Hillary went on *[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes)* to rebuff the charges.[^cite_note-78] Their television appearance was a calculated risk, but Clinton regained several delegates. He finished second to Tsongas in the [New Hampshire primary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election_in_New_Hampshire), but after trailing badly in the polls and coming within single digits of winning, the media viewed it as a victory. News outlets labeled him "The Comeback Kid" for earning a firm second-place finish.[^cite_note-79]

Winning the big prizes of Florida and Texas and many of the [Southern primaries](179553.md) on [Super Tuesday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Tuesday) gave Clinton a sizable delegate lead. However, former California governor [Jerry Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerry_Brown) was scoring victories and Clinton had yet to win a significant contest outside his native South.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14][^cite_note-The_Choice-77] With no major Southern state remaining, Clinton targeted New York, which had many delegates. He scored a resounding victory in New York City, shedding his image as a regional candidate.[^cite_note-The_Choice-77] Having been transformed into the consensus candidate, he secured the Democratic Party nomination, finishing with a victory in Jerry Brown's home state of California.[^cite_note-First_in_His_Class-14]

During the campaign, questions of [conflict of interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_of_interest) regarding state business and the politically powerful [Rose Law Firm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rose_Law_Firm), at which Hillary Rodham Clinton was a partner, arose. Clinton argued the questions were moot because all transactions with the state had been deducted before determining Hillary's firm pay.[^cite_note-80] Further concern arose when Bill Clinton announced that, with Hillary, voters would be getting two presidents "for the price of one".[^cite_note-81]

Clinton was still the governor of Arkansas while campaigning for U.S. president, and he returned to his home state to see that [Ricky Ray Rector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricky_Ray_Rector) would be executed. After killing a police officer and a civilian, Rector shot himself in the head, leading to what his lawyers said was a state where he could still talk but did not understand the idea of death. According to both Arkansas state law and federal law, a seriously mentally impaired inmate cannot be executed. The courts disagreed with the allegation of grave mental impairment and allowed the execution. Clinton's return to Arkansas for the execution was framed in an article for *[The New York Times](30680.md)* as a possible political move to counter "soft on crime" accusations.[^cite_note-mbsrgd-59][^cite_note-Applebome1992-64]



Clinton debating [Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush) and [Perot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Perot) on October 15, 1992

Bush's [approval ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_approval_rating) were around 80 percent during the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), and he was described as being unbeatable. When Bush compromised with Democrats to try to lower federal deficits, he reneged on his [promise not to raise taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Read_my_lips:_no_new_taxes), which hurt his approval rating. Clinton repeatedly condemned Bush for making a promise he failed to keep.[^cite_note-The_Choice-77] By election time, the economy was souring and Bush saw his approval rating plummet to just slightly over 40 percent.[^cite_note-The_Choice-77][^cite_note-82] Finally, conservatives were previously united by anti-communism, but with the end of the Cold War, the party lacked a uniting issue. When [Pat Buchanan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Buchanan) and [Pat Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Robertson) addressed Christian themes at the [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Republican_National_Convention)—with Bush criticizing Democrats for omitting God from their platform—many moderates were alienated.[^cite_note-83] Clinton then pointed to his moderate, "New Democrat" record as Governor of Arkansas, though some on the more liberal side of the party remained suspicious.[^cite_note-84] Many Democrats who had supported Ronald Reagan and Bush in previous elections switched their support to Clinton.[^cite_note-BBConthisday-85] Clinton and his running mate, [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore), toured the country during the final weeks of the campaign, shoring up support and pledging a "new beginning".[^cite_note-BBConthisday-85]

On March 26, 1992, during a Democratic [fund raiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fundraising) of the presidential campaign, [Robert Rafsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Rafsky) confronted then Gov. Bill Clinton of Arkansas and asked what he was going to do about [AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS), to which Clinton replied, "I feel your pain".[^cite_note-86] The televised exchange led to AIDS becoming an issue in the 1992 presidential election. On April 4, then candidate Clinton met with members of [ACT UP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACT_UP) and other leading AIDS advocates to discuss his AIDS agenda and agreed to make a major AIDS policy speech, to have people with HIV speak to the [Democratic Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_National_Convention), and to sign onto the [AIDS United](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AIDS_United) Action five point plan.[^cite_note-87]

Results for the 1992 United States presidential election

1992 electoral vote results. Clinton won 370–168.

Clinton won the [1992 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_United_States_presidential_election) (370 electoral votes) against Republican incumbent George H. W. Bush (168 electoral votes) and billionaire [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism) [Ross Perot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Perot) (zero electoral votes), who ran as an independent on a platform that focused on domestic issues. Bush's steep decline in public approval was a significant part of Clinton's success.[^cite_note-BBConthisday-85] Clinton's victory in the election ended twelve years of Republican rule of the White House and twenty of the previous twenty-four years. The election gave Democrats full control of the [United States Congress](31756.md),[^cite_note-whitehouse.gov_bio-4] the first time one party controlled both the executive and legislative branches since Democrats held the [96th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/96th_United_States_Congress) during the [presidency of Jimmy Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jimmy_Carter).[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89]

According to [Seymour Martin Lipset](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seymour_Martin_Lipset), the 1992 election had several unique characteristics. Voters felt that economic conditions were worse than they actually were, which harmed Bush. A rare event was the presence of a strong third-party candidate. Liberals launched a backlash against 12 years of a conservative White House. The chief factor was Clinton's uniting his party, and winning over a number of heterogeneous groups.[^cite_note-90]

### 1996 presidential candidacy

Main articles: [Bill Clinton 1996 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton_1996_presidential_campaign), [1996 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries), and [1996 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_presidential_election)

Results for the 1996 United States presidential election

1996 electoral vote results. Clinton won 379–159.

Leading up to the [1996 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_presidential_election), Clinton's chances of being re-elected initially seemed slim, partially due to his growing untrust among the general public due to the [Whitewater controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewater_controversy)[^cite_note-91][^cite_note-92] and the lopsided defeat of national Democrats in the 1994 elections.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] His [approval rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Approval_rating) got as low as 40 percent in early 1995, which led to several high-profile Democrats suggesting he drop out of the race.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96] However, in mid-1995, as a result of a rebounding economy and the growing unpopularity of congressional Republicans, public opinion of Clinton up-ticked[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-98][^cite_note-99] and early 1996 polls found he had a lead of up to 20 points over his likely Republican opponent [Bob Dole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dole).[^cite_note-100]

Unlike Bush in the 1992 election, Clinton's incumbency greatly benefited him in the general election,[^cite_note-101] as most Americans felt the country was going in the right direction.[^cite_note-102] Along with Dole, Clinton once again faced Ross Perot, who was nominated by the [Reform Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reform_Party_of_the_United_States_of_America), but he garnered significantly less support than he did in the 1992 election.[^cite_note-103] In the month leading up the election, pundits were predicting [a big win](41053306.md) for Clinton, as his approval rating saw a high of 60 percent[^cite_note-104] and pollsters finding he was favored with voters in over 30 states.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107]

On election day, Clinton won 379 [electoral votes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_College_\(United_States\)), securing reelection and defeating Dole, who received 159 electoral votes.[^cite_note-108] Clinton garnered 49.2 percent of the popular vote to Dole's 40.7 percent and Perot's 8.4 percent. With his victory, he became the first Democrat to win two consecutive presidential elections since [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt).[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-Clinton\@2-110]

## Presidency (1993–2001)

Main article: [Presidency of Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bill_Clinton)

Clinton's "[third way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way#United_States)" of moderate liberalism built up the nation's fiscal health and put the nation on a firm footing abroad amid globalization and the development of anti-American terrorist organizations.[^cite_note-111]

During his presidency, [Clinton advocated for a wide variety of legislation and programs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Bill_Clinton#Legislation_and_programs), most of which were enacted into law or implemented by the executive branch. His policies, particularly the [North American Free Trade Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement) and [welfare reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_Responsibility_and_Work_Opportunity_Act), have been attributed to a [centrist](39619035.md) [Third Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Way) philosophy of governance.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] His policy of [fiscal conservatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_conservatism) helped to reduce deficits on budgetary matters.[^cite_note-wp070914-114][^cite_note-bbc010115-115] Clinton presided over the longest period of peacetime economic expansion in American history.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-NYTExpansion-117] "The Republican response in Clinton's first term was the scorched earth policies of Newt Gingrich, achieved through the threat of government shut-downs." - [Stanley N. Katz, Ph. D.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Nider_Katz)[^cite_note-118]

The [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office) reported budget surpluses of \$69 billion in 1998, \$126 billion in 1999, and \$236 billion in 2000,[^cite_note-119] during the last three years of Clinton's presidency.[^cite_note-120] Over the years of the recorded surplus, the gross national debt rose each year. At the end of the fiscal year (September 30) for each of the years a surplus was recorded, the U.S. Treasury reported a gross debt of \$5.413 trillion in 1997, \$5.526 trillion in 1998, \$5.656 trillion in 1999, and \$5.674 trillion in 2000.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122] Over the same period, the Office of Management and Budget reported an end of year (December 31) gross debt of \$5.369 trillion in 1997, \$5.478 trillion in 1998, \$5.606 in 1999, and \$5.629 trillion in 2000.[^cite_note-123] At the end of his presidency, the Clintons moved to 15 Old House Lane in [Chappaqua, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chappaqua,_New_York), in order to quell political worries about his wife's residency for election as a U.S. senator from New York.[^cite_note-124]

### First term (1993–1997)

> "Our democracy must be not only the envy of the world but the engine of our own renewal. There is nothing wrong with America that cannot be cured by what is right with America."

— Inaugural address, January 20, 1993.

[^cite_note-125]

Clinton during the signing of the Israel–Jordan peace treaty, with Yitzhak Rabin (left) and King Hussein of Jordan (right)

Clinton during the signing of the [Israel–Jordan peace treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel%E2%80%93Jordan_peace_treaty), with [Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin) (left) and King [Hussein of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_of_Jordan) (right)

After [his presidential transition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_transition_of_Bill_Clinton), Clinton was [inaugurated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Bill_Clinton) as the 42nd president of the United States on January 20, 1993. Clinton was physically exhausted at the time, and had an inexperienced staff. His high levels of public support dropped in the first few weeks, as he made a series of mistakes. His first noimnee for [Attorney General](44002.md), [Zoë Baird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zo%C3%AB_Baird), had to withdraw after it came to light that she had employed an undocumented immigrant as her child's nanny; his second nominee for the position, [Kimba Wood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimba_Wood), had to withdraw [for the same reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nannygate). Clinton had repeatedly promised to encourage [gays in the military service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gays_in_the_military), despite what he knew to be the strong opposition of the military leadership. He tried anyway, and was publicly opposed by the top generals, and forced by Congress to a compromise position of "[Don't ask, don't tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_ask,_don't_tell)" whereby homosexuals were allowed serve only if they kept their sexuality a secret.[^cite_note-126] He devised a \$16-billion stimulus package primarily to aid inner-city programs desired by liberals, but it was defeated by a Republican [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster) in the Senate.[^cite_note-127] His popularity at the 100 day mark of his term was the lowest of any president at that point.[^cite_note-128]

Public opinion did support one liberal program, and Clinton signed the [Family and Medical Leave Act of 1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_and_Medical_Leave_Act_of_1993), which required large employers to allow employees to take unpaid leave for pregnancy or a serious medical condition. This action had bipartisan support,[^cite_note-129] and was popular with the public.[^cite_note-130]

Two days after taking office, on January 22, 1993—the 20th anniversary of the U.S. Supreme Court decision in *[Roe v. Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roe_v._Wade)*—Clinton reversed restrictions on domestic and international [family planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Family_planning) programs that had been imposed by Reagan and Bush.[^cite_note-131] Clinton said [abortion](765.md) should be kept "safe, legal, and rare"—a slogan that had been suggested by political scientist [Samuel L. Popkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_L._Popkin) and first used by Clinton in December 1991, while campaigning.[^cite_note-132] During the eight years of the Clinton administration, the abortion rate declined by 18 percent.[^cite_note-133]

On February 15, 1993, Clinton made his first address to the nation, announcing his plan to raise taxes to close a [budget deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_deficit).[^cite_note-134] Two days later, in a nationally televised address to a [joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_session_of_Congress), Clinton unveiled his economic plan. The plan focused on reducing the deficit rather than on cutting taxes for the middle class, which had been high on his campaign agenda.[^cite_note-135] Clinton's advisers pressured him to raise taxes, based on the theory that a smaller federal budget deficit would reduce bond interest rates.[^cite_note-136]

President Clinton's attorney general [Janet Reno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Janet_Reno) authorized the FBI's use of armored vehicles to deploy tear gas into the buildings of the [Branch Davidian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branch_Davidians) community near [Waco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waco,_Texas), Texas, in hopes of ending a [51 day siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waco_siege). During the operation on April 19, 1993, the buildings caught fire and 75 of the residents died, including 24 children. The raid had originally been planned by the Bush administration; Clinton had played no role.[^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138]

In August, Clinton signed the [Omnibus Budget Reconciliation Act of 1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omnibus_Budget_Reconciliation_Act_of_1993), which passed Congress without a Republican vote. It cut taxes for 15 million low-income families, made tax cuts available to 90 percent of small businesses,[^cite_note-139] and raised taxes on the wealthiest 1.2 percent of taxpayers. Additionally, it mandated that the budget be balanced over many years through the implementation of spending restraints.[^cite_note-140]



Clinton and Vice President [Al Gore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Gore) on the South Lawn, August 10, 1993

On September 22, 1993, Clinton made a major speech to Congress regarding [a health care reform plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_health_care_plan_of_1993); the program aimed at achieving universal coverage through a national health care plan. This was one of the most prominent items on Clinton's legislative agenda and resulted from a task force headed by Hillary Clinton. The plan was well received in political circles, but it was eventually doomed by well-organized lobby opposition from conservatives, the [American Medical Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Medical_Association), and the health insurance industry. However, Clinton biographer [John F. Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Harris) said the program failed because of a lack of coordination within the White House.[^cite_note-The_Survivor-58] Despite the Democratic majority in Congress, the effort to create a national health care system ultimately died when compromise legislation by [George J. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_J._Mitchell) failed to gain a majority of support in August 1994. The failure of the bill was the first major legislative defeat of the Clinton administration.[^cite_note-The_Natural-55][^cite_note-The_Survivor-58]

On November 30, 1993, Clinton signed into law the [Brady Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brady_Handgun_Violence_Prevention_Act), which mandated federal [background checks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Background_check) on people who purchase firearms in the United States. The law also imposed a five-day waiting period on purchases, until the [NICS system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Instant_Criminal_Background_Check_System) was implemented in 1998. He also expanded the [Earned Income Tax Credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earned_Income_Tax_Credit), a subsidy for low-income workers.[^cite_note-The_Survivor-58]

In December of the same year, allegations by Arkansas state troopers [Larry Patterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Patterson) and Roger Perry were first reported by [David Brock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Brock) in *[The American Spectator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_American_Spectator).* In the affair later known as "[Troopergate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troopergate_\(Bill_Clinton\))", the officers alleged that they had arranged sexual liaisons for Clinton when he was Governor of Arkansas. The story mentioned a woman named *Paula*, a reference to [Paula Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paula_Jones). Brock later apologized to Clinton, saying the article was politically motivated "bad journalism", and that "the troopers were greedy and had slimy motives".[^cite_note-apology-141]



[Yitzhak Rabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yitzhak_Rabin), Clinton and [Yasser Arafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yasser_Arafat) during the [Oslo Accords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oslo_Accords) on September 13, 1993

That month, Clinton implemented a Department of Defense directive known as "[Don't Ask, Don't Tell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell)", which allowed gay men and women to serve in the armed services provided they kept their sexual orientation a secret. The Act forbade the military from inquiring about an individual's sexual orientation.[^cite_note-142] The policy was developed as a compromise after Clinton's proposal to allow gays to serve openly in the military met staunch opposition from prominent congressional Republicans and Democrats, including senators [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain) (R-AZ) and [Sam Nunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Nunn) (D-GA). According to [David Mixner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Mixner), Clinton's support for the compromise led to a heated dispute with Vice President Al Gore, who felt that "the President should lift the ban ... even though \[his executive order] was sure to be overridden by the Congress".[^cite_note-Mixner2009-143] Some gay-rights advocates criticized Clinton for not going far enough and accused him of making his campaign promise to get votes and contributions.[^cite_note-144] Their position was that Clinton should have integrated the military by executive order, noting that President [Harry S. Truman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_S._Truman) used executive order to racially desegregate the armed forces. Clinton's defenders argued that an executive order might have prompted the Senate to write the exclusion of gays into law, potentially making it harder to integrate the military in the future.[^cite_note-The_Natural-55] Later in his presidency, in 1999, Clinton criticized the way the policy was implemented, saying he did not think any serious person could say it was not "out of whack".[^cite_note-145] The policy remained controversial, and was finally [repealed in 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don't_Ask,_Don't_Tell_Repeal_Act_of_2010), removing open sexual orientation as a reason for dismissal from the armed forces.[^cite_note-146]

On January 1, 1994, Clinton signed the North American Free Trade Agreement into law.[^cite_note-NAFTA-147] Throughout his first year in office, Clinton consistently supported ratification of the treaty by the U.S. Senate. Clinton and most of his allies in the Democratic Leadership Committee strongly supported free trade measures; there remained, however, strong disagreement within the party. Opposition came chiefly from anti-trade Republicans, protectionist Democrats and supporters of Ross Perot. The bill passed the house with 234 votes in favor and 200 votes opposed (132 Republicans and 102 Democrats in favor; 156 Democrats, 43 Republicans, and one independent opposed). The treaty was then ratified by the Senate and signed into law by the president.[^cite_note-NAFTA-147]

On July 29, 1994, the Clinton administration launched the first official White House website, [whitehouse.gov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitehouse.gov).[^cite_note-\:1-148] The site was followed with three more versions, with the final version being launched on July 21, 2000.[^cite_note-\:1-148] The White House website was part of a wider movement of the Clinton administration toward web-based communication. According to Robert Longley, "Clinton and Gore were responsible for pressing almost all federal agencies, the U.S. court system and the U.S. military onto the Internet, thus opening up America's government to more of America's citizens than ever before. On July 17, 1996, Clinton issued Executive Order 13011—Federal Information Technology, ordering the heads of all federal agencies to utilize information technology fully to make the information of the agency easily accessible to the public."[^cite_note-149]

The [Omnibus Crime Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violent_Crime_Control_and_Law_Enforcement_Act), which Clinton signed into law in September 1994,[^cite_note-150] made many changes to U.S. crime and law enforcement legislation including the expansion of the death penalty to include crimes not resulting in death, such as running a large-scale drug enterprise. During Clinton's re-election campaign he said, "My 1994 crime bill expanded the death penalty for drug kingpins, murderers of federal law enforcement officers, and nearly 60 additional categories of violent felons."[^cite_note-151] It also included a subsection of [assault weapons ban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assault_Weapons_Ban) for a ten-year period.[^cite_note-152]

After two years of Democratic Party control, the Democrats lost control of Congress to the Republicans in the [mid-term elections in 1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections), for the first time in forty years.[^cite_note-153]

A speech delivered by President Bill Clinton at the December 6, 1995 [White House Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Conference) on HIV/AIDS projected that a cure for AIDS and a vaccine to prevent further infection would be developed. The President focused on his administration's accomplishments and efforts related to the [epidemic](66981.md), including an accelerated drug-approval process. He also condemned [homophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homophobia) and discrimination against people with [HIV](14170.md). Clinton announced three new initiatives: creating a special working group to coordinate AIDS research throughout the [federal government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_government); convening public health experts to develop an action plan that integrates HIV prevention with substance abuse prevention; and launching a new effort by the [Department of Justice](52563.md) to ensure that health care facilities provide equal access to people with HIV and AIDS.[^cite_note-154] 1996 would mark the first year since the beginning of the [HIV/AIDS epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS_epidemic) that the number of new HIV/AIDS diagnoses would decline, with the U.S. [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) (CDC) even later reporting a significant 47% decline in the number of AIDS-related deaths in 1997 compared to the previous year.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-clintonhhs-156][^cite_note-responsetohivaids-157] Credit for this decline would be given to the growing effectiveness of new drug therapy which was promoted by the Clinton Administration's Department of Health and Human Services, such as [highly active antiretroviral therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highly_active_antiretroviral_therapy) (HAART).[^cite_note-clintonhhs-156][^cite_note-responsetohivaids-157]



Clinton's coat of arms, granted by the [Chief Herald of Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Herald_of_Ireland) in 1995

On September 21, 1996, Clinton signed into law the [Defense of Marriage Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defense_of_Marriage_Act) (DOMA), which defined marriage for federal purposes as the legal union of one man and one woman; the legislation allowed individual states to refuse to recognize gay marriages that were performed in other states.[^cite_note-158] [Paul Yandura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Yandura), speaking for the White House gay and lesbian liaison office, said Clinton's signing DOMA "was a political decision that they made at the time of a re-election". In defense of his actions, Clinton has said that DOMA was intended to "head off an attempt to send a constitutional amendment banning gay marriage to the states", a possibility he described as highly likely in the context of a "very reactionary Congress".[^cite_note-159] Administration spokesman [Richard Socarides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Socarides) said, "the alternatives we knew were going to be far worse, and it was time to move on and get the president re-elected."[^cite_note-metroweekly-160] Clinton himself said DOMA was something "which the Republicans put on the ballot to try to get the base vote for Bush up, I think it's obvious that something had to be done to try to keep the Republican Congress from presenting that";[^cite_note-161] others were more critical. The veteran gay rights and gay marriage activist [Evan Wolfson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Wolfson) has called these claims "historic revisionism".[^cite_note-metroweekly-160] Despite this, it has been noted that other than a brief written response to a *Reader's Digest* that questioned whether he agreed with it, Clinton had made no documented reference to the issue of gay marriage until May 1996.[^cite_note-162] In a July 2, 2011, editorial *The New York Times* opined, "The Defense of Marriage Act was enacted in 1996 as an election-year wedge issue, signed by President Bill Clinton in one of his worst policy moments."[^cite_note-163] Ultimately, in *[United States v. Windsor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_v._Windsor)*, the U.S. Supreme Court struck down DOMA in June 2013.[^cite_note-164]

Despite DOMA, Clinton was the first president to select openly gay persons for administrative positions,[^cite_note-165] and he is generally credited as being the first president to publicly champion gay rights.[^cite_note-newyorker.com-166] During his presidency, Clinton issued two substantially controversial executive orders on behalf of gay rights, the first lifting the ban on security clearances for LGBT federal employees[^cite_note-167] and the second outlawing discrimination based on sexual orientation in the federal civilian workforce.[^cite_note-168] Under Clinton's leadership, federal funding for HIV/AIDS research, prevention and treatment more than doubled.[^cite_note-169] Clinton also pushed for passing hate crimes laws for gays and for the private sector [Employment Non-Discrimination Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Employment_Non-Discrimination_Act), which, buoyed by his lobbying, failed to pass the Senate by a single vote in 1996.[^cite_note-170] Advocacy for these issues, paired with the politically unpopular nature of the gay rights movement at the time, led to enthusiastic support for Clinton's election and reelection by the [Human Rights Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Campaign).[^cite_note-newyorker.com-166] Clinton came out for gay marriage in July 2009[^cite_note-171] and urged the Supreme Court to overturn DOMA in 2013.[^cite_note-172] He was later honored by [GLAAD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GLAAD) for his prior pro-gay stances and his reversal on DOMA.[^cite_note-173]

> "When I took office, only high energy physicists had ever heard of what is called the Worldwide Web ... Now even [my cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socks_\(cat\)) has its own page."

— Bill Clinton's announcement of [Next Generation Internet initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Next_Generation_Internet_Program), October 1996.

[^cite_note-174]

The [1996 United States campaign finance controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_United_States_campaign_finance_controversy) was an alleged effort by [China](5405.md) to influence the domestic policies of the United States, before and during the Clinton administration, and involved the fundraising practices of the administration itself.[^cite_note-wsjchinagate-175][^cite_note-176] Despite the evidence,[^cite_note-wsjchinagate-175][^cite_note-177] the [Chinese government](23239.md) denied all accusations.[^cite_note-embassy-178]

As part of a 1996 initiative to curb [illegal immigration](5044573.md), Clinton signed the [Illegal Immigration Reform and Immigrant Responsibility Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_Immigration_Reform_and_Immigrant_Responsibility_Act_of_1996) (IIRIRA) on September 30, 1996. Appointed by Clinton,[^cite_note-179] the [U.S. Commission on Immigration Reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_laws_concerning_immigration_and_naturalization_in_the_United_States#1990s) recommended reducing legal immigration from about 800,000 people a year to about 550,000.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181]

In November 1996, Clinton narrowly escaped possible assassination in the Philippines,[^cite_note-youtube.com-182] which was a bridge bomb planted by [al-Qaeda](1921.md) and was masterminded by [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden). During Clinton's presidency, the attempt remained top secret,[^cite_note-183] and it remains classified as of March 2024, when [Reuters](18998750.md) reported having spoken with eight retired secret service agents about the incident.[^cite_note-184]

### Second term (1997–2001)



Clinton (center) with other world leaders attending the first [G8 Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G8_Summit), 1997



Clinton with British Prime Minister [Tony Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Blair), Chinese President [Jiang Zemin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jiang_Zemin), French President [Jacques Chirac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Chirac), and Russian President [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) at the [Waldorf Astoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldorf_Astoria_New_York) on September 7, 2000

In the January 1997 State of the Union address, Clinton proposed a new initiative to provide health coverage to up to five million children. Senators [Ted Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy)—a Democrat—and [Orrin Hatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orrin_Hatch)—a Republican—teamed up with Hillary Rodham Clinton and her staff in 1997 and succeeded in passing legislation forming the [State Children's Health Insurance Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Children's_Health_Insurance_Program) (SCHIP), the largest successful health care reform in the Clinton Administration. That year, Hillary Clinton shepherded through Congress the [Adoption and Safe Families Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adoption_and_Safe_Families_Act) and two years later she succeeded in helping pass the [Foster Care Independence Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foster_Care_Independence_Act). Bill Clinton negotiated the passage of the [Balanced Budget Act of 1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balanced_Budget_Act_of_1997) by the Republican Congress.

In October 1997, Clinton announced he was getting hearing aids, due to hearing loss attributed to his age, and his time spent as a musician in his youth.[^cite_note-185] In 1999, he signed into law the Financial Services Modernization Act also known as the [Gramm–Leach–Bliley Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gramm%E2%80%93Leach%E2%80%93Bliley_Act), which repealed the part of the [Glass–Steagall Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glass%E2%80%93Steagall_Act) that had prohibited a bank from offering a full range of [investment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investment_banking), [commercial banking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commercial_bank), and insurance services since its enactment in 1933.[^cite_note-186]

#### Investigations

In November 1993, [David Hale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Hale_\(Whitewater\))—the source of criminal allegations against Bill Clinton in the Whitewater controversy—alleged that while Governor of Arkansas, Clinton pressured Hale to provide an illegal \$300,000 loan to [Susan McDougal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_McDougal), the Clintons' partner in the Whitewater land deal.[^cite_note-salon031798-187] A [U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Securities_and_Exchange_Commission) investigation resulted in convictions against the McDougals for their role in the Whitewater project, but the Clintons themselves were never charged, and Clinton maintains his and his wife's innocence in the affair.[^cite_note-188] Investigations by [Robert B. Fiske](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_B._Fiske) and [Ken Starr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Starr) found insufficient to evidence to prosecute the Clintons.[^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190]

The [White House FBI files controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_FBI_files_controversy) of June 1996 arose concerning improper access by the White House to [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) security-clearance documents. Craig Livingstone, head of the White House Office of Personnel Security, improperly requested, and received from the FBI, background report files without asking permission of the subject individuals; many of these were employees of former Republican administrations.[^cite_note-191] In March 2000, Independent Counsel [Robert Ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Ray_\(prosecutor\)) determined there was no credible evidence of any crime. Ray's report further stated, "there was no substantial and credible evidence that any senior White House official was involved" in seeking the files.[^cite_note-192]

On May 19, 1993, Clinton fired seven employees of the White House Travel Office. This caused the [White House travel office controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_travel_office_controversy) even though the travel office staff served at the pleasure of the president and could be [dismissed without cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/At-will_employment). The White House responded to the controversy by claiming that the firings were done in response to financial improprieties that had been revealed by a brief FBI investigation.[^cite_note-193] Critics contended that the firings had been done to allow friends of the Clintons to take over the travel business and the involvement of the FBI was unwarranted.[^cite_note-194] The House Government Reform and Oversight Committee issued a report which accused the Clinton administration of having obstructed their efforts to investigate the affair.[^cite_note-cnn_com-195] Special counsel Robert Fiske said that Hillary Clinton was involved in the firing and gave "factually false" testimony to the GAO, congress, and the independent counsel. However Fiske said there was not enough evidence to prosecute.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-cnn_com-195]

#### Impeachment and acquittal

Main articles: [Impeachment of Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Bill_Clinton) and [Impeachment trial of Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_trial_of_Bill_Clinton)



Clinton's impeachment trial in 1999

After [a House inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_inquiry_into_Bill_Clinton), Clinton was [impeached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_impeachment_in_the_United_States) on December 19, 1998, by the House of Representatives. The House voted 228–206 to impeach him for perjury to a [grand jury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_jury)[^cite_note-ai-197] and voted 221–212 to impeach him for obstruction of justice.[^cite_note-aiii-198] Clinton was only the second U.S. president (the first being [Andrew Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Johnson)) to be impeached.[^cite_note-199] Impeachment proceedings were based on allegations that Clinton had illegally lied about and covered up his relationship with 22-year-old White House (and later [Department of Defense](7279897.md)) employee [Monica Lewinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monica_Lewinsky).[^cite_note-200] After the [Starr Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starr_Report) was submitted to the House providing what it termed "substantial and credible information that President Clinton Committed Acts that May Constitute Grounds for an Impeachment",[^cite_note-201] the House began impeachment hearings against Clinton before the [mid-term elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_midterm_election). To hold impeachment proceedings, Republican leadership called a [lame-duck session](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lame-duck_session) in December 1998.

While the [House Judiciary Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_Judiciary_Committee) hearings ended in a straight party-line vote, there was lively debate on the House floor. The two charges passed in the House (largely with Republican support, but with a handful of Democratic votes as well) were for perjury and obstruction of justice. The perjury charge arose from Clinton's testimony before a grand jury that had been convened to investigate perjury he may have committed in his sworn deposition during *[Jones v. Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_v._Jones),* Paula Jones's sexual harassment lawsuit.[^cite_note-202] The obstruction charge was based on his actions to conceal his relationship with Lewinsky before and after that deposition.

The Senate later acquitted Clinton of both charges.[^cite_note-senvoteai-203] The Senate refused to meet to hold an impeachment trial before the end of the old term, so the trial was held over until the next Congress. Clinton was represented by Washington law firm [Williams & Connolly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williams_&_Connolly).[^cite_note-204] The Senate finished a twenty-one-day trial on February 12, 1999, with the vote of 55 not guilty/45 guilty on the perjury charge[^cite_note-senvoteai-203] and 50 not guilty/50 guilty on the obstruction of justice charge.[^cite_note-washingtonpostimpeachment-205] Both votes fell short of the constitutional two-thirds majority requirement to convict and remove an officeholder. The final vote was generally along party lines, with no Democrats voting guilty, and only a handful of Republicans voting not guilty.[^cite_note-senvoteai-203]

On January 19, 2001, Clinton's law license was suspended for five years after he acknowledged to an Arkansas circuit court he had engaged in conduct prejudicial to the administration of justice in the *Jones* case.[^cite_note-arkcomm-206][^cite_note-wsj01plea-207] On October 1, the U.S. Supreme Court suspended Clinton from practicing law in the high court, citing fallout from the Lewinsky scandal,[^cite_note-208] but rather than appealing the decision he resigned from the bar entirely.[^cite_note-209]

#### Pardons and commutations

Clinton [issued](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton_pardon_controversy) 141 pardons and 36 commutations on his last day in office on January 20, 2001.[^cite_note-The_Survivor-58][^cite_note-210] Controversy surrounded [Marc Rich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Rich) and allegations that Hillary Clinton's brother, [Hugh Rodham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Edwin_Rodham), accepted payments in return for influencing the president's decision-making regarding the pardons.[^cite_note-211] Federal prosecutor [Mary Jo White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Jo_White) was appointed to investigate the pardon of Rich. She was later replaced by then-Republican [James Comey](2156690.md). The investigation found no wrongdoing on Clinton's part.[^cite_note-212] Clinton also pardoned four defendants in the [Whitewater Scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitewater_Scandal), [Chris Wade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Wade_\(real_estate_broker\)), [Susan McDougal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_McDougal), [Stephen Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Smith_\(Whitewater\)), and [Robert W.
